answer with a prerequisite, and the\n> prerequisite is large.  Recorded here so the adjudication prices it rather than assuming it.\n\n*** THE NOTE ABOVE UNDERSTATED THE SCOPE, AND THE CORRECTION IS THE USEFUL PART: FAULT IS NOT\n\"UNAVAILABLE ON ALPHA\".  IT IS AVAILABLE ON **MIPS AND M88K ONLY**, FOR EVERY ARCHITECTURE IN THIS\nTREE.  Derived 2026-09-08 by opening the dispatcher. ***\n\n`src/cpus/memory_rw.c`, the device-failure path -- the whole of it:\n\n    if (res <= 0 && !no_exceptions) {\n            debug(\"[ %s device '%s' addr %08lx failed ]\");\n    #ifdef MEM_MIPS\n            mips_cpu_exception(cpu, cache == CACHE_INSTRUCTION? EXCEPTION_IBE : EXCEPTION_DBE, ...);\n    #endif\n    #ifdef MEM_M88K\n            m88k_exception(cpu, cache == CACHE_INSTRUCTION ? M88K_EXCEPTION_INSTRUCTION_ACCESS\n                                                           : M88K_EXCEPTION_DATA_ACCESS, 0);\n    #endif\n            return MEMORY_ACCESS_FAILED;\n    }\n\n**TWO `#ifdef` ARMS.  There is no ARM arm, no Alpha arm, no PPC arm and no SH arm** -- every other\narchitecture reaches `return MEMORY_ACCESS_FAILED` with no exception delivered at all.\n\n> **SO OPTION-SET MEMBER 5 IS A PHANTOM ON EVERY ARCHITECTURE BUT TWO**, and a round on an ARM, SH,\n> PPC or Alpha device that reaches for FAULT is reaching for a round that does not exist yet.\n\n*Found because I wrote the OPPOSITE into a brief.  Assessing `fbfiqsilent` -- an ARM footbridge row\n-- I told the seat that \"member 5 FAULT may genuinely be available here, `memory_rw`'s\ndevice-failure path has an ARM arm.\"  It replied that the claimed arm **is absent from this tree**,\nand opening the file confirms it.  **I was one brief away from spreading a false availability claim\nacross the ARM queue**, having just corrected three rows for the Alpha version of it.*\n\n*** codex: REVISE.  THE SHIFT IS RIGHT AND ALMOST EVERYTHING AROUND IT NEEDED CORRECTING --\nINCLUDING TWO PREMISES I STATED AS SETTLED. ***\n\n**CONFIRMED, with a denominator.**  `dev = (tag >> 11) & 0x1f` is correct: `relative_addr >> 5`\nundoes the address expansion, so for a valid tag T and aligned register R,\n`A = (T << 5) | (R << 5) | 24` gives `((A >> 5) & ~0xff) == T`, putting device bits at 15..11 of\nthe recovered tag.  Validated over **4,194,304 encode/decode combinations, zero mismatches** --\nand codex labelled that an ARITHMETIC check, not an emulator test, which is the honest form.\n\n*** MY IMPLEMENT SKETCH WAS INCOMPLETE: IT DECODED `dev` AND NOT `bus`. ***  `IOC_CONF` selects\nthe CYCLE FORMAT, it does not supply the bus number -- PCI address bits 23..2 come from CPU\naddress bits 28..7, and only bits 1..0 come from `IOC_CONF[1:0]`.  So the minimum correction is\n\n    } else {\n        bus = (tag >> 16) & 0xff;\n        dev = (tag >> 11) & 0x1f;\n    }\n\n> **AND THE TRAP IN THE OBVIOUS ALTERNATIVE, which I would have walked into: DO NOT CHOOSE THE\n> DECODING BRANCH FROM THE EXTRACTED BUS FIELD.**  Primary-bus IDSEL bits overlap that field, and\n> keeping `bus == 1` in the forwarding call **collapses every type-1 destination onto bus 1**.\n\n*** PREMISE 4 IS FALSE AS STATED, and the refutation is one function away. ***  I wrote that an\nempty-slot read returns all-ones.  `bus_pci_data_access` implements a THREE-WAY contract:\n\n    read, register 0        ->  0xffffffffffffffff\n    read, any other reg     ->  ZERO\n    write                   ->  a diagnostic on EVERY access, then returns\n\nThe measured ID-register instance stands; the GENERAL claim does not.  **This matters because it\nmeans the candidate dispositions differ TODAY, with no bus-1 device added at all** -- an\nunconditional all-ones responder differs from the backend at nonzero registers, and announcement\npolicies differ on diagnostic output.  (The write diagnostic does not terminate: `fatal()` returns\nnormally.)\n\n*** THE DISCRIMINATING ROW I PROPOSED CANNOT DISCRIMINATE, AND IT HAS THREE FALSE-POSITIVE TRAPS.\n***  At register 0, all-ones is returned by correct forwarding, by the INCORRECT bus-fixed-to-1\nforwarding, by explicit all-ones completion, and by announced all-ones completion -- four\ndispositions, one observable.  Worse:\n\n* **THE EXISTING ADDRESS DOES NOT ENCODE BUS 1.**  `lca_confbus_probe.py`'s setup uses a ZERO\n  window offset, so the recovered tag -- bus field included -- is zero, and the control side\n  reaches the **no-IDSEL fallback to device 0**.  *The witness establishes mode-dependent\n  termination, not a correctly addressed secondary-bus transaction.*  That is a scope correction\n  to what this row's existing witness is entitled to claim.\n* **A FAILED LOAD PRESERVES THE CONTROL SIGNATURE.**  `memory_rw` pre-zeros, so a handler that\n  returns success without filling data yields zero -- but a literal `return 0` takes the FAILURE\n  path, and `LS_GENERIC_N` then returns *before updating the destination register*, so the witness\n  **reads back the all-ones the register already held.**  A vacuity trap of exactly the shape the\n  carrier's witness clauses exist to catch.\n* **THE PARSER TAKES THE FIRST OBSERVATION** (`printed` uses `re.search`), so adding another print\n  and calling the helper again retrieves the CONTROL's result rather than the new one.\n\n**WHAT IT PRESCRIBES INSTEAD:** keep the signature check as an empty-ID COMPLETION check with an\nindependently identified result and a distinct prior destination value, and add a **model-level\nrouting fixture** observing the exact bus/device/function/register passed onward -- distinct\nsynthetic devices on bus 1 and on another bus separate correct decoding from hardcoded bus 1 and\nfrom constant-return stubs.  Include write dispatch and preservation of primary-bus decoding.\n\n*** A SECOND ARCHITECTURAL PREMISE CORRECTED: RAW LCA ABSENCE DOES NOT GUARANTEE ALL-ONES. ***\nDigital's §6.2.18 says an unanswered transaction is a MASTER ABORT: the read returns *arbitrary\ndata with error status*, raising an instruction exception plus interrupt and IOC error logging.\n**So the 64-bit all-ones signature is an EMULATOR-COMPATIBILITY oracle, not a hardware contract**,\nand the round may not claim hardware fidelity for it.  The current path delegates to the generic\nbackend and returns success; it implements none of that error sequence.\n\n**SCOPE, stated so the round does not overclaim what it closes:** this closes the configuration\nhandler's termination and its missing destination decode ONLY.  Still open in the same file --\n`lca_ioc`'s unconditional read/write `default` termination and its register-value rejections;\n`lca_interrupt_assert` / `lca_interrupt_deassert`; and `lca_isa`'s separate guard.  Nor does\nforwarding implement bridge topology or configuration-cycle error reporting: the backend matches a\nnumeric tuple and receives **no cycle-type argument at all**, so type 1 with a zero bus field needs\nan explicit policy or simple forwarding will alias a root-bus device.\n\n**PRIMARY SOURCE, and this project did not have it:** codex cited the *Digital 21066/21066A\nHardware Reference Manual* (Table 6-1, §6.4.2, §6.2.18) with page anchors. CONFIRMED.\n    :9216  S6.2.9   \"PCI address bits ad<1:0> come directly from configuration cycle type\n                     register (IOC_CONF, Section 6.4.2) bits <1:0>\"\n                    -> IOC_CONF selects the CYCLE TYPE and does NOT supply the bus. THE DISCRIMINATING ROW, WHICH IS WHAT BOTH WITNESS CONTRACTS SAID WAS MISSING. ***\n**\"Stopped guest state, plus the reason -- NOT survival.\"**  Proposed acceptance row: starting from\na running CPU, an unsupported LCA assertion produces its specific diagnostic and leaves\n`cpu.running == false`; with debugger-at-end enabled the debugger stays responsive and subsequent\nsteps execute no guest instructions; the matched non-asserting control remains RUNNING.\n\n    diagnosed guest stop                              GREEN\n    silent return                                     RED -- CPU remains running\n    latched announce and return                       RED -- CPU remains running\n    forwarding only, or delivery to a resuming handler RED -- CPU remains running\n    the existing exit(1)                              RED -- debugger/process is gone\n\n**That discriminates all five candidates**, and the state is already observable: `cpu_new()` exposes\n`running` as a setting, `machine_run()` skips stopped CPUs, and `emul_run()` enters the debugger\nafter all machines stop when debugger-at-end is set.\n\n> **SO THE CONTRACT LIMIT ON BOTH LCA WITNESSES IS LIFTED -- for THIS disposition.**  Each says\n> \"host survives\" cannot tell the fix members apart, and that remains true; the answer is that the\n> oracle must read the CPU's RUNNING STATE and the diagnostic, not the host's survival.  The\n> existing probes would need that row added; neither has it today.\n\n**THE FABLE QUESTION IN §7j-1 IS NARROWED BUT NOT ANSWERED.**  I asked whether a latched decline is\nacceptable for an unimplemented delivery path.  codex's answer is that a THIRD option dominates both\nhorns -- but choosing between \"stop the guest with a diagnostic\" and \"lose interrupts quietly and\nkeep running\" is still a judgment about what an emulator owes its user, and that is exactly what the\nflagship seat is held for.  **§7j-2 (what observable discriminates) is now ANSWERED and can be\nstruck from the queue when the adjudication runs.**\n\n*** THE LOAD-BEARING CLAIM WAS VERIFIED INDEPENDENTLY, AND MY FIRST CHECK NEARLY REFUTED IT\nWRONGLY. ***\n\ncodex's whole disposition rests on \"the Alpha CPU cannot take an asynchronous interrupt\". Recorded here so the adjudication prices it rather than assuming it.\n\n*** THE NOTE ABOVE UNDERSTATED THE SCOPE, AND THE CORRECTION IS THE USEFUL PART: FAULT IS NOT\n\"UNAVAILABLE ON ALPHA\".  IT IS AVAILABLE ON **MIPS AND M88K ONLY**, FOR EVERY ARCHITECTURE IN THIS\nTREE.  Derived 2026-09-08 by opening the dispatcher. ***\n\n`src/cpus/memory_rw.c`, the device-failure path -- the whole of it:\n\n    if (res <= 0 && !no_exceptions) {\n            debug(\"[ %s device '%s' addr %08lx failed ]\");\n    #ifdef MEM_MIPS\n            mips_cpu_exception(cpu, cache == CACHE_INSTRUCTION? EXCEPTION_IBE : EXCEPTION_DBE, ...);\n    #endif\n    #ifdef MEM_M88K\n            m88k_exception(cpu, cache == CACHE_INSTRUCTION ? M88K_EXCEPTION_INSTRUCTION_ACCESS\n                                                           : M88K_EXCEPTION_DATA_ACCESS, 0);\n    #endif\n            return MEMORY_ACCESS_FAILED;\n    }\n\n**TWO `#ifdef` ARMS. Assessing `fbfiqsilent` -- an ARM footbridge row\n-- I told the seat that \"member 5 FAULT may genuinely be available here, `memory_rw`'s\ndevice-failure path has an ARM arm.\"  It replied that the claimed arm **is absent from this tree**,\nand opening the file confirms it.  **I was one brief away from spreading a false availability claim\nacross the ARM queue**, having just corrected three rows for the Alpha version of it.*\n\n*** codex: CONFIRMED GUEST-REACHABLE PROCESS TERMINATION ON alphabook1, WITH THE PATH NAMED. ***\n\n    dev_ns16550_access() / scheduled UART tick -> dev_ns16550_tick()\n        -> isa_interrupt_assert() -> isa_interrupt_common() -> lca_interrupt_assert() -> exit(1)\n\n**The decisive wiring is one line after the registration:** `devinit_lca()` passes the same\ninterrupt path to `bus_isa_init()` (`dev_lca.c:418`), which connects its upstream interrupt to the\nsupplied template (`bus_isa.c:190`) and **unconditionally installs the first UART on ISA IRQ 4**\n(`bus_isa.c:250` -- verified by reading: the `ns16550 irq=%s.isa.%i` device_add with `4`).  No\nmachine-description modification is involved, which is what makes it a witness candidate rather\nthan laundering.\n\n**LCA line 0 is the AGGREGATE ISA input, not ISA IRQ 0** -- a correction to my brief's reading of\nthe comment.  And missing Alpha CPU dispatch does not prevent reaching the callback: *the process\nexits before CPU acceptance matters*.\n\n*** THE REPAIR IS EXPLICITLY PARTIAL, AND I VERIFIED THE REASON MYSELF. ***  codex says forwarding\nto an upstream `struct interrupt` is the minimum useful repair but cannot complete delivery,\nbecause the Alpha CPU callbacks only set and clear `cpu->cd.alpha.irq_asserted` and **that field\nhas no reader**.  Checked by census:\n\n    cd.alpha.irq_asserted   written cpu_alpha.c:218, :223   declared cpu_alpha.h:161   READ NOWHERE\n    cd.arm.irq_asserted     READ at cpu_dyntrans.c:208\n    cd.m88k.irq_asserted    READ at cpu_dyntrans.c:212 and :382\n    cd.ppc.irq_asserted     READ at cpu_dyntrans.c:243 and cpu_ppc.c:309\n\n**`cpu_dyntrans.c` has no Alpha arm at all.**  So a correct forwarding fix would set a flag nothing\nconsumes -- honest containment, not delivery.\n\n**FOOTBRIDGE IS THE CLOSEST IN-TREE ANALOGUE and it does NOT transfer literally:** it registers\n`interrupt->line` as a **bit mask** while LCA registers **index zero**, so a copied\n`status |= interrupt->line` would record nothing.\n\n*** MY OWN CITATIONS WERE CONTENTS ENTRIES, WHICH codex CAUGHT. ***  The brief cited manual lines\n`175` and `510` for the interrupt sections; those are TABLE-OF-CONTENTS rows.  The bodies are at\n`:4459` (S4.1.13) and `:10729` (S6.4.13).  *I had read the bodies and then cited the contents.*\n\n*** WITNESS ATTEMPT -- MADE, HARNESS WORKS, AND IT DID **NOT** FIRE.  Recorded as an attempt,\nwhich is what the lifecycle rule asks for. ***\n\n`_scratchpad/lcairq_probe.py`, harness copied from `regress/lca_confbus_probe.py` (pty.fork,\n`-V -x -E alpha -e alphabook1 -M 64 0x20000000:alpha_dummy.bin`).  Addresses derived, not guessed:\n`LCA_ISA_BASE = LCA_PCI_SIO(0x1c0000000) + 0x10000000`, UART at `+0x3f8`, K0SEG for the debugger.\n\n    live        alive   alive   CONTROL: plain step on an unmodified machine          ok\n    devsig      alive   alive   CONTROL: the UART window reads through the same decode ok\n    spin only   alive   alive   CONTROL: free-running self-branch, no UART setup       ok\n    irq assert  alive   died    IER=0x02, MCR=0x08, THR write, then `continue`    *** did not fire ***\n\n**Three controls green and the writes visibly land** (the debugger echoes each `put b`).  Two\nthings were learned and are worth carrying: the 8-byte all-zero dummy decodes as `call_pal\nPAL_halt` and **stops the machine before any device tick can run**, which is why the first attempt\nneeded a planted self-branch (`br r31,-1` = `0xc3ffffff`); and even free-running with IER, MCR and\na THR write in place, no assertion reached the stub.\n\n**THE OPEN QUESTION IS WHETHER A DEBUGGER `put` REACHES `DEVICE_ACCESS(ns16550)` AT ALL.**\n`dev_ns16550.c:353` registers via `memory_device_register`, and this project already records that\ndebugger access can bypass a handler depending on the registration flags.  If `put` writes a shadow\nrather than the device, `IIR_TXRDY` was never set and the tick's condition was never true -- which\nwould make the attempt inconclusive rather than negative.  **That is the next thing to settle, and\nit is a measurement, so it belongs to the reserved measuring seat rather than to another reading.**\n\n*So the row stands on codex's SOURCE-DERIVED reachability, not on an executed witness.  The\ndistinction matters and is recorded rather than blurred.*\n\n*** THE WITNESS ATTEMPT'S OPEN QUESTION IS NOW SETTLED, AND THE ANSWER IS THAT MY APPARATUS COULD\nNOT DO WHAT I ASKED OF IT.  A DEBUGGER `put' NEVER REACHES `DEVICE_ACCESS(ns16550)'. ***\n\n    debugger_cmds.c:1461, :1507   memory_rw(..., MEM_WRITE, CACHE_NONE | NO_EXCEPTIONS)\n    memory_rw.c:584               if (!no_exceptions || (flags & DM_READS_HAVE_NO_SIDE_EFFECTS))\n                                          -> only THEN is devices[i].f() called\n    dev_ns16550.c:353-355         memory_device_register(..., DM_DEFAULT, NULL)\n\n`DM_DEFAULT` is 0, so the UART carries no `DM_READS_HAVE_NO_SIDE_EFFECTS` bit; the debugger passes\n`NO_EXCEPTIONS`; **the handler is skipped.**  My three `put b' writes to IER, MCR and THR were\nabsorbed without ever invoking `dev_ns16550_access`, so `IIR_TXRDY` was never set, the tick's\ncondition was never true, and nothing asserted.\n\n> **SO THE ATTEMPT WAS INCONCLUSIVE FOR AN APPARATUS REASON, AND THAT IS NOW A FACT RATHER THAN A\n> SUSPICION.**  It says nothing whatever about whether the defect is reachable -- codex's\n> source-derived path stands untouched.\n\n*** AND IT GENERALISES INTO A PROBE-WRITING RULE THIS PROJECT HAD ONLY HALF-RECORDED. ***  The\ncarrier already notes that the debugger's `dump` bypasses a `DM_DEFAULT` handler on READS, and\nprescribes guest loads for SH4 state.  **The same gate governs WRITES, by the same line of\n`memory_rw.c`** -- so:\n\n> **CONFIGURING A DEVICE FROM THE DEBUGGER WITH `put' IS A NO-OP AGAINST ANY `DM_DEFAULT' DEVICE.\n> Device access in a probe must be made by GUEST INSTRUCTIONS.**  `put' is for RAM -- scratch\n> pointers, planted code, page tables -- where no handler is involved.\n\n**THE IN-TREE PROBES ALREADY DO THIS CORRECTLY, which is why nothing else is broken:**\n`lca_confbus_probe.py` uses `put d' only to seed SCRATCH in RAM and then reaches the device with\nguest `STQ`/`LDQ`; `alpha_swpctx_v0_probe.py` (#514) uses `put' only for RAM and `call_pal', and\ntouches no device at all.  **My failed attempt is the one that broke the pattern**, and it broke it\nby configuring a device the debugger cannot configure.\n\n**WHAT THE WITNESS NOW NEEDS, concretely:** the UART's IER/MCR/THR must be written by guest stores.\nThe registers are byte-wide at `LCA_ISA_BASE + 0x3f8` with `addrmult = 1`, so this needs either a\nbyte store (`STB`, which is a BWX instruction and may not exist on a 21064-class part -- CHECK\nbefore assuming) or a wider store whose other bytes are harmless.  **That check is the next step\nand it is cheap; it is recorded here rather than guessed.**\n\n*** THE WITNESS FIRES.  `regress/lca_irqstub_probe.py', measured on `2ab3d2f' with `src/'\nUNMODIFIED: 2/4, and the transcript's last line is the stub's own diagnostic. ***\n\n    live         alive   alive   CONTROL: a free-running self-branch alone survives        ok\n    ram ctl      alive   alive   ATTRIBUTION: the IDENTICAL sequence against RAM survives   ok\n    uart irq     died    alive   an ordinary UART interrupt must not end the emulator     FAIL\n    stub named   yes     no      the stub's own TODO diagnostic must not appear           FAIL\n\n    GXemul> continue\n    lca_interrupt_assert: TODO\n\n**THREE GUEST BYTE STORES END THE EMULATOR** -- IER `0x02`, MCR `0x08`, then any byte to THR --\non an unmodified `-E alpha -e alphabook1` with no `device_add` and no machine-description change.\n`WITNESS_HEAD_20260908.out` and the full session transcript are preserved beside it.\n\n*** `ram ctl' IS WHAT MAKES THIS A WITNESS RATHER THAN A CRASH REPORT. ***  It runs the\n**identical instruction sequence** -- same registers, same opcodes, same count -- against RAM\ninstead of the UART, and survives.  So the termination is attributable to the DEVICE and not to the\ncode sequence, the self-branch, or the free-running mode.  One control, two hypotheses separated.\n\n*** IT TOOK THREE FACTS TO GET HERE, AND TWO OF THEM WERE MINE TO GET WRONG. ***\n\n1. **codex's source path**, which named the wiring end to end: `devinit_lca()` hands its interrupt\n   path to `bus_isa_init()`, which unconditionally installs the first ns16550 on ISA IRQ 4.\n2. **The `put'/DM_DEFAULT finding** -- my FIRST attempt configured the UART with debugger `put' and\n   nothing happened, because `put' passes `NO_EXCEPTIONS' and `memory_rw' then skips a `DM_DEFAULT'\n   device's handler entirely.  *That attempt was inconclusive for an apparatus reason, and until it\n   was traced it looked like evidence the path was unreachable.*\n3. **`stb' is ungated in this tree.**  The UART registers are byte-wide, so the guest needs a byte\n   store -- a BWX instruction a real 21066 would not have.  `cpu_alpha_instr.c:895` decodes\n   `case 0x0e` with **no feature check**, and `ALPHA_FEATURE_BWX` (`cpu_alpha.h:57`) is **DEFINED\n   AND NEVER CONSULTED** -- the third declared-but-unused constant found in this header today,\n   after `ALPHA_PTE_ASM` and `ALPHA_KENTRY_MM`.  **So this probe depends on an emulator fact rather\n   than a hardware one, and a future round that gates BWX would break it.**  Recorded in the probe\n   header, not left to be rediscovered.\n\n**SEVERITY: the row is already `high` and this does not raise it -- it removes the last doubt about\nreachability.**  The witness ladder is explicit that rung measures reachability-proof strength and\nnever harm, so a witness landing does not by itself move severity.  *What it does change is that\n\"guest-reachable\" is no longer source-derived.*\n\n**WHAT THE ROW STILL OWES is unchanged and is the hard part:** the disposition.  Forwarding cannot\ncomplete delivery, because `cd.alpha.irq_asserted` has no reader and `cpu_dyntrans.c` has no Alpha\narm -- so any repair converts *\"the emulator terminates\"* into *\"the interrupt is silently\ndropped\"*.  That question is with codex and is queued for the flagship seat.\n\n*** CORRECTION, AND IT IS MINE: THIS ROW WAS ALREADY WITNESSED AND I BUILT A SECOND WITNESS\nWITHOUT CHECKING.  I ALSO OVERWROTE THE COMMITTED PROBE FILE DOING IT. ***\n\n`regress/lca_irqstub_probe.py` shipped in **`7c2f8e4` on 2026-09-07** -- *\"lcairqstubkill\nWITNESSED: four guest instructions end the host through a documented 8042 register\"* -- scoring\n**3/4**, with an LPT control chosen from a codex ISA census because `dev_lpt_tick` is empty and\ncannot assert.  **The ledger's own `witness` field said all of that**, and I replaced it with a\nthinner sentence about my own probe.\n\n**HOW IT WAS CAUGHT: the probe-wiring checker's DUPLICATE-KEY guard.**  A dict literal keeps only\nthe last value and says nothing, so the checker refuses two entries for one key -- and it named\n`lca_irqstub_probe.py`.  Without that guard I would have shipped a manifest that silently\ndescribed the wrong probe.  *Nothing else in the gate would have noticed, because the file existed\nand the row had a witness either way.*\n\n**DAMAGE, checked rather than assumed:** the probe file was overwritten and is **restored from\nHEAD**; the ledger `witness` field is **restored and extended**.  **Yesterday's artifacts are\nintact** -- `witness_HEAD_20260907.raw` is unmodified, and `git diff HEAD` over that directory\nshows only my new files added.\n\n**WHAT SURVIVES, AND IT IS WORTH KEEPING:** my probe is a **SECOND, INDEPENDENT PATH** -- ns16550\non IRQ 4 rather than 8042 on IRQ 1, three byte stores rather than one word store, with its own\nidentical-sequence-against-RAM control.  **Two independent guest routes to one stub is real\nvalue**: a repair that silences one and not the other is visible at once.  It is renamed\n`lca_irqstub_uart_probe.py` and its artifacts `UART_WITNESS_*`, so nothing can be mistaken for the\nrow's witness of record.\n\n**AND TWO FINDINGS FROM BUILDING IT STAND ON THEIR OWN**, independent of the duplication: the\n`put`/`DM_DEFAULT` mechanism (a debugger `put` never reaches a `DM_DEFAULT` device's handler, now\nfiled as a probe-writing hazard), and that `ALPHA_FEATURE_BWX` is defined and never consulted so\n`stb` executes on any Alpha here.\n\n> **THE PROCESS FAILURE IS EXACT AND WORTH NAMING: I read the row's `state_note` and its briefs,\n> and never read its `witness` FIELD.**  The standing instruction is to check the repo before\n> starting, and I checked the prose and not the schema.  *A row that already carries a witness is\n> the cheapest possible thing to check, and it is one field.*\n\n*** THE DISPOSITION IS DESIGNED, WITH TWO TRANSFERRED WARRANTS AND AN EXPLICIT LIMIT. ***\n\n**VERDICT (codex):** *bounded announcement plus level forwarding, as a PARTIAL repair, with Alpha\ninterrupt delivery left explicitly unresolved.*\n\n*** IT ARGUED THE STRONGEST CASE AGAINST THAT BEFORE HOLDING IT, which is what was asked. ***\n*\"It preserves the guest's failure while weakening its visibility.  An interrupt-dependent guest\ncan still hang; a warning can disappear into logs; an automation wrapper can mistake continued\nprocess life for success.  Forwarding adds no presently functioning guest capability because\nnothing consumes the flag.  An announced drop would achieve the same immediate containment with\nless plumbing.\"*\n\n> **AND THEN THE DISCRIMINATION THAT SETTLES IT:** *\"That objection defeats a claim of FIXED ALPHA\n> INTERRUPTS.  It does not defeat the narrower improvement of removing these two process exits.\"*\n> The round may remove the terminations; it may not claim delivery.\n\n**THE ADDITION LICENCE IS SATISFIED BY TWO NAMED IN-TREE PRECEDENTS, not by \"it cannot hurt\":**\nFootbridge's connection-and-propagation shape (`dev_footbridge.c:794`), and -- the one that matters\nfor the announce -- **`dev_pcc2.c:369`, which ALREADY ANNOUNCES AN UNAVAILABLE INTERRUPT-DELIVERY\nMODE ONCE.**  That is this exact disposition, already shipped elsewhere in the tree.\n\n**THE MINIMAL IMPLEMENTATION, concretely:** add `struct interrupt irq;` and `int\nirq_delivery_warned;` to `lca_data` (the existing `memset` initialises the latch);\n`INTERRUPT_CONNECT(devinit->interrupt_path, d->irq)` before creating downstream devices, since that\npath already defaults to the bootstrap CPU; a `lca_interrupt_notice()` helper carrying the `(once)`\nmarker; then `notice(); INTERRUPT_ASSERT(d->irq);` and the deassert mirror.  **N = 1 per instance\nwith an explicit suppression marker -- the house idiom, not the counter form.**\n\n*** IT ALSO CORRECTED ITS OWN EARLIER FOUR-ROW COVERAGE TABLE as having \"overstated what could\nimmediately become passing coverage\", which is a seat being harder on itself than on me: ***\n\n    callback forwarding      BUILDABLE TODAY as a host-side test; the current callbacks fail it\n                             and the small repair passes it\n    masking and release      SPLIT.  Level retention/withdrawal passes after the small repair;\n                             masked-versus-unmasked ENTRY requires CPU delivery, and no entry in\n                             either case proves nothing about masking\n    vector acknowledgement   BLOCKED on missing LCA acknowledgement support -- a SEPARATE missing\n                             dependency, implementable independently of CPU dispatch\n    guest execution          CANNOT pass with forwarding alone\n\n**SO THE ROUND'S DETECTOR IS THE FIRST ROW PLUS HALF THE SECOND**, and the record must say that\nrather than implying the table.\n\n*** AND THE DELIVERY ROUND IS BIGGER THAN A CONDITIONAL IN `cpu_dyntrans.c'. ***  codex found two\nmore declared-but-unused things in this layer: **the tree WRITES `kentry[]` AND NEVER CONSUMES\nIT**, and **`PAL_OSF1_rti` has a printable name with no execution implementation** -- so the PAL\ndispatcher cannot supply the interrupt entry/return lifecycle at all.  *That is the fifth\ndeclared-but-unused Alpha item found today, after `ALPHA_PTE_ASM`, `ALPHA_KENTRY_MM` and\n`ALPHA_FEATURE_BWX`.*  Queued as its own compatibility round with a named measured delta: on the\npartial-repair baseline, an eligible asserted input leaves a guest handler counter unchanged, and\nthe implementation makes the handler execute and return.\n\n**Footbridge supplies no Alpha PAL entry convention**, so that warrant does NOT transfer to the\ndelivery round -- only to the propagation shape.\n\n*** THE SIBLING CHECK THE ERROR DEMANDED, AND IT CAME BACK CLEAN. ***  Having overwritten one\ncommitted witness, the obvious question is whether I have done it anywhere else, or whether any\nother row's `witness' field names something that is no longer there.  Censused across every OPEN\nrow:\n\n    open rows                                        194\n    of which ALREADY CARRY A WITNESS                 119\n    whose witness names a MISSING file                 0\n\n**Zero.**  Every path named in a witness field exists on disk, so this was a single incident and\nthe restore closed it.  *And all EIGHT high-severity open rows already carry a witness* -- which\nsays something about the queue that is worth writing down: **the top of this queue is not short of\nevidence, it is short of DISPOSITIONS.**  That is the shape of work the flagship adjudication is\nfor, and it is why the packet's questions are disposition questions rather than requests for more\nmeasurement.\n\n*** THE DISPOSITION QUESTION IS SETTLED, 2026-09-08, AND THE ANSWER IS THAT FORWARDING IS A NO-OP ON\nTHIS ARCHITECTURE.  Led by the codex seat, VERIFIED BY ME IN ONE GREP. ***\n\nThe round had been parked on *\"forwarding alone leaves Alpha delivery unresolved\"* without anyone\nestablishing what Alpha delivery actually IS.  A codex ANALYSIS job (see `codexcyberflag` -- the\nconstruction-shaped brief is refused, the analysis-shaped one is not) said mid-stream:\n\n> *\"Alpha has a CPU interrupt pin and an `irq_asserted` field, but the searches so far show only\n> WRITES to that field.\"*\n\n**Its stream then ended without a final verdict, so this would have been an UNREAD REVIEW had the\n`.err` not been opened.  Checked rather than taken:**\n\n    grep -rn irq_asserted src/          ->  57 references, classified\n\n    cpu_alpha.c:218    cpu->cd.alpha.irq_asserted = 1;      WRITE\n    cpu_alpha.c:223    cpu->cd.alpha.irq_asserted = 0;      WRITE\n    cpu_alpha.h:161    int irq_asserted;                    DECL\n    ---------------------------------------------------------------------------\n    READS OF cpu->cd.alpha.irq_asserted ANYWHERE IN src/ :   ZERO\n\n`cpu_dyntrans.c` is where a pending interrupt becomes a delivered one, and it tests exactly three\narchitectures -- `cd.arm.irq_asserted` (`:208`), `cd.m88k.irq_asserted` (`:212`, `:382`) and\n`cd.ppc.irq_asserted` (`:243`).  **Alpha is absent from that list.**  `cpu_ppc.c:309` shows the\nsame field being consumed on the PPC side; there is no Alpha counterpart.\n\n> **SO THE ALPHA CPU MODEL HAS AN INTERRUPT-PENDING FLAG THAT NOTHING CONSUMES.  Wiring the LCA\n> stubs to `cpu_interrupt()` would set a field no code reads: a NO-OP, not a delivery.**\n\n*** WHAT THIS SETTLES, and it changes the round from blocked to shippable: ***\n\n* **Member 7 (IMPLEMENT) is NOT a wiring job.**  It requires adding Alpha interrupt dispatch to\n  `cpu_dyntrans.c` plus the PALcode entry and IPL check that go with it -- a SUBSYSTEM, and its own\n  round.  The earlier note's instinct that *\"declining an interrupt leaves the machine incomplete\"*\n  was right about the machine and wrong about the size of the gap: the machine is already\n  incomplete, before this row's stubs are reached.\n* **The partial repair is COHERENT, which was the open question.**  Nothing in the model blocks\n  waiting for an acknowledgement that cannot come, because nothing is waiting at all -- there is no\n  consumer.  Replacing `exit(1)` with a bounded announce leaves the guest with interrupts silently\n  dropped, which is EXACTLY what it already gets on every Alpha path that does not terminate.\n* **The honest disclosure the round owes** is therefore not *\"we chose not to forward\"* but\n  *\"Alpha interrupt delivery does not exist in this CPU model; these stubs cannot forward to\n  anything, and the fix removes a host termination without pretending to add delivery.\"*\n\n**AND IT REFRAMES THE SEVERITY HONESTLY IN BOTH DIRECTIONS.**  The termination is still\nguest-reachable and still witnessed twice (8042 command port, 3/4; ns16550 IRQ 4, 2/4), so the row\nstays HIGH.  But the fix buys *the host stops dying*, NOT *interrupts start working* -- and a round\nblock claiming the latter would be a wrong record.\n\n*Provenance worth keeping: this is the reading-seat-buys-the-class pattern exactly.  codex named the\nfamily (`only writes to that field`) and never finished its answer; one grep turned that into a\nmeasured fact that settles a design question two panels had left open.  The cost of NOT opening a\nzero-byte job's `.err` would have been another round of design argument.*\n\n*** PASS 1, 2026-09-08.  P4 SURVIVED A SEAT THAT TRIED TO REFUTE IT, AND FOUR OF MY BRIEF'S OTHER\nCLAIMS DID NOT. ***\n\n**grok: \"RATIFY member 3 (per-instance, two bits), do not forward, do not call this delivery.\nP4 holds and is STRONGER than the brief states.\"**  On Q1 specifically: *\"Yes. I tried to refute it\nand failed.  Delivery does not exist.\"*  **deepseek** conditionally accepted and did the correct\npacket-fed thing -- refused to ratify P4 as unverifiable from the packet and named the exact search\nthat settles it, rather than agreeing.\n\n*** THE FOUR CORRECTIONS, three of which change the round. ***\n\n**(1) Q2 WAS MIS-FRAMED AND THE QUESTION CANNOT BE ASKED.**  I asked whether the stubs should write\n`irq_asserted` anyway.  grok: *\"`interrupt->extra` is `struct lca_data *`, NOT a `struct cpu *`.\nThere is no `irq_asserted` in this file to write.             low-order bits of the CPU physical addresses that reference PCI address spaces
            are encoded as follows:

            � Physical address bits <6:3> generate the PCI byte enable c_be_l<3:0>
                  signals. Byte enables generated by combinations of physical address bits
                  <6:3>, not shown in Table 6-3, are unspecified.

    � Physical address bits <4:3> also specify transfer size.

    � Physical address bits <2:0> must be zero for transfers in sparse space.

    Table 6-3 shows how the low-order physical address bits are used to indicate
    transfer size.

Table 6-3 Byte Enable Encoding for CPU-Initiated Transfers in Sparse Space

3Byte enables are active low (asserted when shown as zero).

ad2 is 0 for the first longword and 1 for the second longword of the quadword. The quadword is normally
sent as a burst of two consecutive longwords, starting with the lower longword; in this case, only one address
(ad2 = 0) is generated. If a retry on the second longword is needed, the address would be regenerated with
ad2 = 1.

    During CPU-initiated transfers to sparse memory space, the IOC forces PCI
    address bits <1:0> to 00. This indicates to the target device that the transfer
    will use a linearly incrementing burst order.

    Although the PCI supports arbitrary byte enables, the IOC supports only the
    byte-enable combinations listed in Table 6-3.

    Masked Transfers: The following types of masked transfers are permitted in
    sparse space:

    � Longword read transfers--An LDL instruction can generate a masked PCI
         read transfer with a burst length of one.

    � Longword write transferss--An STL instruction must be used. The STL
         instruction generates a PCI write transfer with a burst length of one.

6-8 I/O Controller
            Unmasked Transfers: The following types of unmasked transfers are
            permitted in sparse space:

            � Longword read transfers--An LDL instruction can generate an unmasked
                  PCI read transfer with a burst length of one.

            � Quadword read transfers--An LDQ instruction can generate an unmasked
                  PCI read transfer with a burst length of two.

            � Longword write transfers--An STL instruction can generate an unmasked
                  PCI write transfer with a burst length of one.

            � Quadword write transfers--An STQ instruction can generate an unmasked
                  PCI write transfer with a burst length of two.

6.2.6.2 Address Extension for Sparse Space
            Because the CPU address is encoded to generate PCI byte enables, only 27
            physical address bits (CPU address bits a<31:5>) can be used to generate PCI
            address bits ad<26:0>, giving an effective PCI address space of 128 MB. This
            128-MB address space is subdivided into a 16-MB region and a 112-MB region.
            Generation of PCI address bits ad<31:27> depends on which of these regions
            are referenced, as shown in Table 6-4.

Table 6-4 Host Address Extension

   CPU       CPU         PCI
a<33:32>  a<31:29>  ad<31:27>

10        000       00000

10        001       IOC_HAE<31:27>

10        010       IOC_HAE<31:27>

10        011       IOC_HAE<31:27>

10        100       IOC_HAE<31:27>

10        101       IOC_HAE<31:27>

10        110       IOC_HAE<31:27>

10        111       IOC_HAE<31:27>

The 16-MB region is always mapped to the first 16 MB of the PCI address
space. This 16-MB region is referenced when CPU address bits a<31:29> are
zero. During CPU-initiated PCI transactions to this 16-MB region, PCI address
bits ad<31:27> are zero.

The 112-MB region is referenced when CPU address bits a<31:29> are non-
zero. During CPU-initiated PCI transactions to this 112-MB region, PCI
address bits ad<31:27> are generated using the host address extension (HAE)
field in the IOC_HAE register (Section 6.4.1). The HAE field allows the 112-MB
region to be relocated to the upper 112 MB of any aligned 128-MB PCI address

                                    I/O Controller 6-9
            segment. A value of zero in the HAE field defines a single 128-MB region,
            beginning at address 0 in PCI address space.

6.2.6.3 Burst Order in Sparse Space
            For sparse memory space, PCI address bits <1:0> in memory cycles specify
            the burst order requested by the bus master. The microprocessor always uses
            a linearly incrementing burst order. Only a CPU-initiated PCI unmasked-
            quadword read or write cycle can generate a transfer with a burst length
            greater than one (Section 6.2.6.6). For dense space, bursts can be greater than
            one.

6.2.6.4 Unmasked Longword and Quadword Read Operations to Sparse Space
            CPU-initiated PCI longword read operations are generated by executing LDL
            or LDQ instructions that reference a physical address in the PCI sparse
            memory space. Encoded CPU address bits <6:5> must equal 00 for a longword
            and 11 for a quadword; CPU address bits <4:3> must equal 11, as specified in
            Table 6-3.

            Address Phase: In the address phase of a CPU-initiated PCI longword
            or quadword read cycle, the IOC drives a memory read command on the
            c_be_l<3:0> pins.

            Address Phase + 1: In the cycle following the address phase of a longword
            read cycle, the IOC deasserts the frame_l signal, asserts the PCI initiator
            ready signal irdy_l, and begins to sample the target ready signal trdy_l and
            address signals ad<31:0>. When the IOC samples the trdy_l signal asserted,
            it latches the read data from the ad<31:0> pins.

            In the cycle following the address phase of a quadword read cycle, the IOC
            deasserts the frame_l signal after it samples the trdy_l signal asserted.

            Address Phase + 2: In the second cycle following the address phase of a
            longword read cycle, the IOC deasserts its irdy_l signal and samples the
            parity data that the target is driving on the par line. The IOC returns the
            longword of read data from the PCI to the CPU in the low longword of the
            returned quadword.

            In the second cycle following the address phase of a quadword read cycle, the
            IOC latches both the upper longword on the ad<31:0> pins and the parity
            associated with the first longword. Parity associated with the second longword
            will appear in the third cycle. The IOC packs the two longwords of read data
            into the single quadword that is returned to the CPU.

            If the parity calculated by the IOC does not match the sampled parity, the IOC
            uses an internal interrupt to report the error to the CPU (Section 6.2.13.1).

6-10 I/O Controller
            Deassertion of the irdy_l signal indicates cycle completion to the PCI target
            device.

6.2.6.5 Unmasked Longword Write Operations to Sparse Space
            CPU-initiated PCI longword write operations are generated by executing STL
            instructions that reference a physical address in the PCI sparse memory space.
            Encoded CPU address bits <6:5> must equal 00 and bits <4:3> must equal 11,
            as specified in Table 6-3.

            Address Phase: In the address phase of a CPU-initiated PCI longword write
            cycle, the IOC drives a memory write command on the c_be_l<3:0> pins.

            Address Phase + 1: In the cycle following the address phase, the IOC does
            the following:

            � Deasserts the frame_l signal

            � Asserts the PCI initiator ready signal irdy_l

            � Drives valid write data on the ad<31:0> pins

            � Begins to sample the target ready signal trdy_l

            Address Phase + 2: In the second cycle following the address phase, the IOC
            drives even parity for the ad<31:0> and c_be_l<3:0> signals on the par pin.

            Target Ready + 1: In the cycle after the IOC samples the trdy_l signal
            asserted, it deasserts its irdy_l signal, completing the longword write
            transaction.

            If the target of the write transaction detects a parity error, it asserts the
            perr_l signal two cycles after it asserts the trdy_l signal. If the IOC samples
            the perr_l signal asserted in this cycle (two cycles after the data transfer), it
            uses an internal interrupt to report a parity error to the CPU (Section 6.2.13.2).

            The IOC extracts the longword of write data for the PCI from the CPU
            quadword. The upper or lower longword is indicated by CPU physical address
            bit a7 (0 = low, 1 = high), which corresponds to PCI address bit ad2.

6.2.6.6 Unmasked Quadword Write Operations to Sparse Space
            CPU-initiated PCI quadword write operations are generated by executing STQ
            instructions that reference a physical address in the PCI sparse memory space.
            Encoded CPU address bits <6:5> must equal 11 and bits <4:3> must equal 11,
            as specified in Table 6-3.

            The IOC always transfers the low longword of the quadword write data first,
            followed by the high longword.

                                                                                                               I/O Controller 6-11
            Address Phase: In the address phase of a CPU-initiated PCI quadword write
            cycle, the IOC drives a memory write command on the c_be_l<3:0> pins.
            To complete the quadword write cycle, the IOC initiates a 2-longword burst
            write cycle by using linearly incrementing burst order on the PCI.
            Address Phase + 1: In the cycle following the address phase, the IOC does
            the following:
            � Continues to assert the frame_l signal
            � Asserts the PCI initiator ready signal irdy_l
            � Drives the lower longword of write data on the ad<31:0> pins
            � Begins to sample the target ready signal trdy_l
            Address Phase + 2: In the second cycle following the address phase, the IOC
            drives even parity for the ad<31:0> and c_be_l<3:0> signals on the par pin.
            First Target Ready + 1: In the cycle after the IOC samples the trdy_l signal
            asserted, the IOC does the following:

9104:6.2.6.8 Masked Longword Write Operations to Sparse Space
9105-            CPU-initiated PCI longword write operations are generated by executing
9106-            STL instructions that reference a physical address in the PCI sparse memory
9107-            space. Encoded CPU address bits <4:3> must equal 00, 01, or 10. The encoded
9108-            address determines the byte mask used during the transfer, as specified in
9109-            Table 6�3.
9110-
9111-            Masked longword write operations are similar to unmasked longword write
9112-            operations (Section 6.2.6.4), except that when the IOC is asserting the irdy_l
9113-            signal, it is also asserting only the byte enables specified by the encoded
9114-            address.
9115-
9116:6.2.7 Dense Memory Space
9117-
9118-            The dense memory space is the region in which only unmasked longword or
9119-            quadword transfers can be specified. This memory space uses the onchip write
9120-            buffer more efficiently by condensing multiple quadword transfers into a single
9121-            burst.
9122-
9123:6.2.7.1 Generating PCI Addresses for Dense Space
9124-            Dense memory space uses the unshifted CPU address bits a<31:0> to generate
9125-            the PCI address. Because this region has sufficient address space, the
9126-            IOC_HAE register is not needed or used to generate PCI addresses. Note that
9127-            any PCI address can be generated from dense space. Sparse space address
9128-            generation must follow the address extension described in Section 6.2.6.2.
9129-
9130-            During CPU-initiated transfers to dense memory space, the IOC forces PCI
9131-            address bits ad<1:0> to 00. This indicates to the target device that the transfer
9132-            will use a linearly incrementing burst order.
9133-
9134-            Because the address is not shifted, masked transfers are not permitted in
9135-            dense space.
9136-
9137-                                                                                                               I/O Controller 6�13
9138-6.2.7.2 Unmasked Read Operations to Dense Space
9139-            The PCI returns an aligned quadword in response to a longword read
9140-            operation, regardless of the address alignment. The correct longword is
9141-            returned to the register file in the low 32 bits.
9142-            The PCI returns an aligned quadword in response to a quadword read
9143-            operation. The address is assumed to be quadword aligned.
9144-
9145:6.2.7.3 Unmasked Burst Write Operations to Dense Space
9146-            Unmasked PCI write operations can transfer up to 1 hexword by executing a
9147-            combination of STL and STQ instructions with addresses that fall within an
9148-            aligned hexword. All longwords of the hexword need not be written; that is,
9149-            the software can write any longword portion of the hexword--the IOC simply
9150-            sequences through an unwritten longword by deasserting the byte enables for
9151-            that longword.
9152-            The IOC sequences through successive longwords by using a linearly
9153-            incrementing burst order on the PCI.
9154-            The IOC always transfers the low longword of a quadword first, followed by the
9155-            high longword.
9156-            Address Phase + 1: In the cycle following the address phase, the IOC does
9157-            the following:
9158-            � Continues to assert the frame_l signal
9159-            � Asserts the PCI initiator ready signal irdy_l
9160-            � Drives the first longword of write data on the ad<31:0> pins
9161-            � Begins to sample the target ready signal trdy_l
9162-            First Target Ready + 1: In the cycle after the IOC samples the trdy_l signal
9163-            asserted, the IOC does the following:
9164-            � Drives the next longword of write data on the ad<31:0> pins
9165-            � Deasserts its frame_l signal
9166-            � Continues to assert its irdy_l signal
9167-            This process continues until the last longword of the transaction.
9168-
9169-6�14 I/O Controller
9170-            Second Target Ready + 1: In the cycle after the IOC again samples the
9171-            trdy_l signal asserted, it deasserts its irdy_l signal, completing the burst.
9172-
9173-            If the target of the write transaction detects a parity error on a longword,
9174-            it asserts the perr_l signal two cycles after it asserts the trdy_l signal
9175-            corresponding to the data transfer that caused the parity error. If the IOC
9176-            samples the perr_l signal asserted two cycles after a data transfer, it uses an
9177-            internal interrupt to report a parity error to the CPU (Section 6.2.13.2).
9178-
9179:6.2.8 I/O Space Cycles
9180-
9181-            The IOC generates addresses in this region for transactions to the I/O region
9182-            specified by the PCI architecture. This region supports the same access types
9183-            and masked-address generation as sparse space (Section 6.2.6), with the
9184-            following exceptions:
9185-
9186-            � I/O space is accessed with CPU address bits a<33:32> = 01 and
9187-                  a<31:29> = 110.
9188-
9189-            � PCI address bits ad<31:27> = 00000 and the IOC_HAE register is not
9190-                  used.
9191-
9192-            � PCI address bits ad<1:0> come directly from physical address bits a<6:5>
9193-                  during CPU-initiated PCI transfers to I/O space.
9194-
9195-            � An I/O read or write command (Table 6�2) is issued on the c_be_l<3:0>
9196-                  pins coincident with a read or write operation to this region.
9197-
9198-            The I/O region supports all masked and unmasked transfers that are supported
9199-            in sparse space.
9200-
9201-6.2.9 Configuration Cycles
9202-
9203-            The PCI architecture specifies a configuration address space for defining
9204-            configuration registers that are used to initialize and configure devices.
9205-            The IOC supports transfers to the configuration address space to access
9206-            the configuration registers. This region supports the same access types and
9207-            masked address generation as sparse space (Section 6.2.6), with the following
9208-            exceptions:
9209-
9210-            � Configuration space is accessed with CPU address bits a<33:32> = 01 and
9211-                  a<31:29> = 111.
9212-
9213-            � PCI address bits ad<31:27> = 00000 and the IOC_HAE register is not
9214-                  used.
9215-
9216-            � PCI address bits ad<1:0> come directly from configuration cycle type
9217-                  register (IOC_CONF, Section 6.4.2) bits <1:0> during CPU-initiated PCI
9218-                  transfers to configuration space.
9219-
9220-                                                                                                               I/O Controller 6�15
9221-            � A configuration space read or write command (Table 6�2) is issued on the
9222-                  c_be_l<3:0> pins coincident with a read or write operation to this region.
9223-
9224-            During configuration cycles, addresses are restricted both by the encoding
9225-            requirements of PCI configuration cycles (as described in the PCI Local Bus
9226-            Specification, Revision 2.0) and by the system-dependent implementation of
9227-            PCI initialization device select (IDSEL) encoding. Configuration cycles use one
9228-            cycle of address stepping; that is, the address and command are driven for two
9229-            consecutive cycles.
9230-
9231-6.2.10 Special Cycles and Interrupt Acknowledge Cycles
9232-
9233-            The PCI special cycle command implements a simple broadcast mech-
9234-            anism that allows the broadcast of processor status information and
9235-            sideband signaling. The command is not addressed to a specific device,
9236-            and the receivers determine whether the message applies to them.
9237-            Section 6.3.11 describes two supported sideband signals. Also see the
9238-            PCI Local Bus Specification, Revision 2.0 for more information.
9239-
9240-6.2.10.1 Special Cycle
9241-            The CPU can initiate a special cycle transfer on the PCI by executing an STL
9242-            instruction to the interrupt vector and special cycle register (IOC_IACK_SC,
9243-            Section 6.4.13). When this register is written, the IOC initiates a PCI cycle
9244-            with a special cycle bus command. During a special cycle, the IOC generates
9245-            an unspecified address on the PCI that is ignored by the PCI devices. The
9246-            write data generated by the STL instruction passes unmodified through the
9247-            IOC to the PCI during the data phase of the special cycle. The special cycle
9248-            message is encoded in the write data as described in the PCI Local Bus
9249-            Specification, Revision 2.0.
9250-
9251-            The special cycle address region is accessed only by STL instructions. This
9252-            region supports the same write access types and masked address generation as
9253-            sparse space (Section 6.2.6), with the following exceptions:
9254-
9255-            � Write access to the IOC_IACK_SC register is enabled when CPU address
9256-                  bits a<33:32> = 01 and a<31:29> = 101.
9257-
9258-            � The IOC_HAE register is used to generate PCI address bits ad<31:27>
9259-                  under all circumstances.
9260-
9261-            � A special cycle command (Table 6�2) is issued on the c_be_l<3:0> pins, and
9262-                  a PCI interrupt acknowledge cycle follows as a result.
9263-
9264-6�16 I/O Controller

9268-            arbitration hardware to supply interrupt vectors for PCI devices.

 succeeded in 249ms:
	int writeflag, uint64_t paddr, size_t len)
{
	/*
	 *  HACK: This allows guest OS kernels to probe memory a few KBs past
	 *  the end of memory, without giving too many warnings.
	 */
	if (paddr < mem->physical_max + 0x40000)
		return false;

	int verbosity = VERBOSITY_WARNING;
	if (cpu->machine->halt_on_nonexistant_memaccess)
		verbosity = VERBOSITY_ERROR;

	// ERROR is always "enough", so we will print in that case.
	if (!ENOUGH_VERBOSITY(SUBSYS_MEMORY, verbosity))
		return false;

	uint64_t offset;
	char* symbol = get_symbol_name(&cpu->machine->symbol_context, cpu->pc, &offset);

	// Not the prettiest, but should work fine.
	if (cpu->is_32bit)
		debugmsg_cpu(cpu, SUBSYS_MEMORY,
		    writeflag ? "WRITE" : "READ",
		    verbosity, "%s non-existant "
			"paddr=0x%08" PRIx32 " len=%i pc=0x%08" PRIx32 "%s%s%s",
		    writeflag ? "to" : "from",
		    (uint32_t) paddr,
		    len,
		    (uint32_t) cpu->pc,
		    symbol ? " <" : "",
		    symbol ? symbol : "",
		    symbol ? ">" : "");
	else
		debugmsg_cpu(cpu, SUBSYS_MEMORY,
		    writeflag ? "WRITE" : "READ",
		    verbosity, "%s non-existant "
			"paddr=0x%016" PRIx64 " len=%i pc=0x%016" PRIx64 "%s%s%s",
		    writeflag ? "to" : "from",
		    (uint64_t) paddr,
		    len,
		    (uint64_t) cpu->pc,
		    symbol ? " <" : "",
		    symbol ? symbol : "",
		    symbol ? ">" : "");

	if (cpu->machine->halt_on_nonexistant_memaccess) {
		// Halt all CPUs, even though the bad memory access was on
		// just one of them.
		for (int i = 0; i < cpu->machine->ncpus; ++i)
			cpu->machine->cpus[i]->running = 0;

		return true;
	}

	return false;
}


/*
 *  dump_mem_string():
 *
 *  Dump the contents of emulated RAM as readable text.  Bytes that aren't
 *  readable are dumped in [xx] notation, where xx is in hexadecimal.
 *  Dumping ends after DUMP_MEM_STRING_MAX bytes, or when a terminating
 *  zero byte is found.
 */
#define DUMP_MEM_STRING_MAX	45
void dump_mem_string(struct cpu *cpu, uint64_t addr)
{
	int i;
	for (i=0; i<DUMP_MEM_STRING_MAX; i++) {
		unsigned char ch = '\0';

		cpu->memory_rw(cpu, cpu->mem, addr + i, &ch, sizeof(ch),
		    MEM_READ, CACHE_DATA | NO_EXCEPTIONS);
		if (ch == '\0')
			return;
		if (ch >= ' ' && ch < 126)
			debug("%c", ch);  
		else
			debug("[%02x]", ch);
	}
}


/*
 *  store_byte():
 *
 *  Stores a byte in emulated ram. (Helper function.)
 */
void store_byte(struct cpu *cpu, uint64_t addr, uint8_t data)
{
	if ((addr >> 32) == 0)
		addr = (int64_t)(int32_t)addr;
	cpu->memory_rw(cpu, cpu->mem,
	    addr, &data, sizeof(data), MEM_WRITE, CACHE_DATA);
}


/*
 *  store_string():
 *
 *  Stores chars into emulated RAM until a zero byte (string terminating
 *  character) is found. The zero byte is also copied.
 *  (strcpy()-like helper function, host-RAM-to-emulated-RAM.)
 */
void store_string(struct cpu *cpu, uint64_t addr, const char *s)
{
	do {
		store_byte(cpu, addr++, *s);
	} while (*s++);
}


/*
 *  add_environment_string():
 *
 *  Like store_string(), but advances the pointer afterwards. The most
 *  obvious use is to place a number of strings (such as environment variable
 *  strings) after one-another in emulated memory.
 */
void add_environment_string(struct cpu *cpu, const char *s, uint64_t *addr)
{
	store_string(cpu, *addr, s);

Same script, opposite outcome -- which is\nthe ladder's clause (i) exactly.\n\n**WHAT THE ROUND OWES, and the one real decision in it:**\n\nThe two-direction control above is a UBSAN DIFFERENTIAL, and this project has that filed as its own\nround ([[ubsanshiftlane]]) because it is a NEW MEASUREMENT LANE -- a second binary and a STDERR\noracle rather than a value oracle.  **So the honest choice is:**\n\n* **(a) ship the fix with the UBSAN differential as its detector**, which means building at least a\n  minimal version of the lane in this round -- and a lane added inside a fix round is a lane nobody\n  reviewed, which is the exact objection `ubsanshiftlane` was filed to avoid; or\n* **(b) ship the fix with a SOURCE PIN** -- a check asserting the expression is `0x800U` -- and\n  leave the differential to its own round.  `#510`'s own product comments already name a source pin\n  as the legitimate alternative to UBSAN for precisely this class (\"No row can kill a `1U' -> `1'\n  regression; UBSAN or a source pin can\").\n\n**(b) is in scope and (a) is not**, on this project's own stopping rule: the fix is a one-token\ncorrectness repair, and expanding it into a new measurement lane is the scope creep the rule exists\nto refuse.  *Recorded as a reasoned choice rather than a preference, so a reviewer can disagree with\nthe reason.*\n\n**COST: a build.**  The edit must propagate to all four trees and `build-asan` must be REBUILT for\nthe post-fix measurement, which is the only expensive part and is why this was not executed the\nmoment it was designed -- a rebuild must not overlap a running seat.\n\n**AND THE POST-FIX RUN IS THE ONE THAT MATTERS MORE THAN THE FIX.**  If the diagnostic does NOT\ndisappear, the repair is wrong or the site is not the one being reported, and that is a finding\nabout the measurement rather than a formality.  Predict-then-measure: it should vanish, and nothing\nelse in the probe's four rows should move (3/4 before, 3/4 after -- the `bus kill` row is a\ndifferent defect and stays RED).\n\n*** FIXED AND MEASURED IN BOTH DIRECTIONS, 2026-09-07, as `#511`. ***\n\n    HEAD    1 diagnostic     ubsan_HEAD_20260907.raw\n    FIXED   0 diagnostics    ubsan_FIXED_20260907.raw\n    control  build/ scores 3/4 before AND after, same three rows green, `bus kill' still RED\n             (a different defect) -- NOTHING ELSE MOVED\n\n**The pre-registered prediction held exactly**: the diagnostic vanished and no other row moved.  All\nfour trees cleared two-part after the propagate (`est/`: source tree, part 2 N/A).\n\n**The detector is the differential itself and it is NOT GATE-WIRED**, which the CHANGELOG block says\nin those words.  A source pin was considered and rejected: it would test the TEXT while this round\nhas the BEHAVIOUR.  Gate-wiring is [[ubsanshiftlane]], deliberately separate.\n\n*** THE SIBLING QUESTION IS ANSWERED BEFORE THE PANEL ANSWERS IT, AND THE RESULT IS A CLEAN\nABSENCE -- RECORDED WITH ITS WARRANT SO IT IS FALSIFIABLE. ***\n\n`#511`'s own pass-2 brief asks (Q5) whether a sibling of this shape was missed.  Censused:\n\n    signed-constant  <<  VARIABLE, tree-wide                       317 sites\n    of those, literal > 1 AND headroom <= 24                          2 sites\n\n**317 IS A CORRELATE, NOT THE CLASS.**  `1 << x` with 31 places of headroom is the ordinary bitmask\nidiom in C and is a defect only if `x` can reach 31.  The `#511` SHAPE is distinctive: a literal\nLARGER than 1 (so little headroom) shifted by a variable that a loop can drive to the overflow point.\nFiltering on that leaves TWO:\n\n    cpu_mips_coproc.c:772   tmp &= 0x0fff << PAGEMASK_SHIFT;    headroom 20\n    cpu_mips_coproc.c:774   tmp &= 0xffff << PAGEMASK_SHIFT;    headroom 16\n\n**BOTH ARE SAFE, and the warrant is a compile-time constant:**\n\n    src/include/cop0.h:85    #define PAGEMASK_SHIFT   13\n    0x0fff << 13 = 0x01ffe000       0xffff << 13 = 0x1fffe000       INT_MAX = 0x7fffffff\n\n`PAGEMASK_SHIFT` is a MACRO, not a runtime variable, so the compiler folds the whole expression and\nthere is no shift to overflow.  **My regex matched an identifier and could not tell a macro from a\nvariable** -- which is why the two hits needed reading rather than counting.\n\n> **[THIS ABSENCE RECORD IS FALSE.  See the correction at the end of this note -- the grok pass-2\n> seat found TWO siblings my filter had hidden.]**  ~~no other site in the tree has a signed literal > 1 shifted by a value that can reach 2^31.~~  THE WARRANT IS\n> `PAGEMASK_SHIFT == 13` AND ITS BEING A COMPILE-TIME CONSTANT.**  If either changes -- the value\n> raised past 15, or the macro replaced by a variable -- the two `cpu_mips_coproc.c` sites become\n> live and this record expires.\n\n*A near-miss worth keeping: my first attempt grepped `define PAGEMASK_SHIFT` in `src/` and returned\nNOTHING, and I had already computed the arithmetic assuming 13.  Zero hits is not evidence, and the\nnumber I was about to rely on was a guess.  It IS 13 -- found in `src/include/cop0.h` on a broader\nsearch -- but the conclusion was reached before the fact was.*\n\n*** THE ABSENCE RECORD ABOVE IS FALSE, AND MY OWN FILTER IS WHAT MADE IT SO.  The grok pass-2 seat\nfound TWO SIBLINGS, both verified here. ***\n\ngrok's verdict on `#511` closes with: *\"Two other PCI IDSEL scanners still evaluate `1 << 31`.\"*\nVerified by reading:\n\n    dev_uninorth.c:70   for (dev=11; dev<32; dev++)\n                 :71       if (idata & (1 << dev))            <- dev REACHES 31\n    dev_v3.c:164        for (dev=24; dev<32; dev++)\n            :165           if (relative_addr & (1 << dev))    <- dev REACHES 31\n\n    1 << 31 = 0x80000000 = 2147483648    INT_MAX = 2147483647    OVERFLOWS\n\n**Both are the IDENTICAL SHAPE to `#511`** -- a signed literal shifted by a loop variable the loop\nbound drives to the overflow point -- and both loops explicitly run to `dev < 32`, so 31 is reached\nwhenever no earlier bit matches.\n\n*** THE FILTER THAT HID THEM WAS MINE, AND IT WAS EXACTLY BACKWARDS. ***  My census excluded\n`if v <= 1: continue`, reasoning that ``1 << x`` is the ordinary bitmask idiom in C and only a\ndefect if `x` reaches 31.  **That reasoning is right and the exclusion is wrong**: `1` has the MOST\nheadroom (31 places), which makes it the ONLY literal that overflows at the maximum shift a\n`< 32` loop can produce.  I filtered on \"little headroom = suspicious\" and thereby removed the\nsingle literal whose headroom exactly matches the commonest loop bound in the tree.\n\n> **AN ABSENCE RECORD IS ONLY AS GOOD AS THE FILTER THAT PRODUCED IT, AND MY WARRANT NAMED THE\n> WRONG THING.**  I recorded the warrant as `PAGEMASK_SHIFT == 13` -- a fact about the two sites the\n> filter DID surface -- when the load-bearing assumption was the FILTER ITSELF.  A warrant should\n> name what would falsify the record, and what falsified it was the exclusion, not the constant.\n\n**The `PAGEMASK_SHIFT` half stands**: those two `cpu_mips_coproc.c` sites really are safe, for the\nreason given.  What is retracted is the universal claim that nothing else in the tree has the shape.\n\n*Filed to [[shiftubcensus]], which is the row for this class and was already marked HONESTLY\nPARTIAL.  Recorded here too because THIS row is where the false absence was written.*\n\n*** codex, `#511` PASS 2, CHALLENGES THE \"GRACEFUL DEGRADATION PRECEDENT\" THAT BOTH LCA ROWS LEAN\nON. ***\n\n*\"`DEVICE_ACCESS(lca_pci_conf)`'s fallback selects device zero and forwards the access through\n`bus_pci_setaddr`.  **That is an ALIASING DECISION, not by itself proof of harmless degradation.**\nThe comment's 'transferable warrant' needs its own justification; the shift repair does not establish\nit.\"*\n\nThe `dev >= 21` arm -- commented-out `fatal()`/`exit(1)` with `dev = 0;` substituted -- has been\ncited repeatedly here, including by [[lcabadval]], as the file's own in-tree warrant for deleting a\nhost kill.  **codex's point is that substituting device ZERO is not a decline: it silently redirects\nthe access to a DIFFERENT DEVICE.**  Whether that is harmless is a separate question nobody has\nasked, and \"someone already did it in this file\" is not the same as \"it is right\".\n\n> **A TRANSFERRED WARRANT NEEDS ITS OWN JUSTIFICATION, NOT JUST A PRECEDENT.**  This is the\n> option-set rule's own caution turned around: grepping the fork for an existing idiom finds what the\n> tree DOES, which is evidence about practice and not about correctness.\n\n*Not resolved here.  Recorded on both rows because both cite it, and any round that leans on it owes\nthe justification codex is asking for.*",

            First Target Ready + 2: In the second cycle after the IOC samples the trdy_l
            signal asserted, the IOC drives even parity for the ad<31:0> and c_be_l<3:0>
            signals on the par pin.
            Second Target Ready + 1: In the cycle after the IOC again samples the
            trdy_l signal asserted, it deasserts its irdy_l signal, completing the quadword
            write transaction.
            If the target of the write transaction detects a parity error on either longword,
            it asserts the perr_l signal two cycles after it asserts the trdy_l signal
            corresponding to the data transfer that caused the parity error. If the IOC
            samples the perr_l signal asserted two cycles after either data transfer, it uses
            an internal interrupt to report a parity error to the CPU (Section 6.2.13.2).

6-12 I/O Controller
6.2.6.7 Masked Longword Read Operations to Sparse Space
            CPU-initiated PCI longword read operations are generated by executing LDL
            instructions that reference a physical address in the PCI sparse memory
            space. Encoded CPU address bits <4:3> must equal 00, 01, or 10. The encoded
            address determines the byte mask used during the transfer, as specified in
            Table 6-3.

            Masked longword read operations are similar to unmasked longword read
            operations (Section 6.2.6.4), except that when the IOC is asserting the irdy_l
            signal, it is also asserting only the byte enables specified by the encoded
            address.

6.2.6.8 Masked Longword Write Operations to Sparse Space
            CPU-initiated PCI longword write operations are generated by executing
            STL instructions that reference a physical address in the PCI sparse memory
            space. Encoded CPU address bits <4:3> must equal 00, 01, or 10. The encoded
            address determines the byte mask used during the transfer, as specified in
            Table 6-3.

            Masked longword write operations are similar to unmasked longword write
            operations (Section 6.2.6.4), except that when the IOC is asserting the irdy_l
            signal, it is also asserting only the byte enables specified by the encoded
            address.

6.2.7 Dense Memory Space

            The dense memory space is the region in which only unmasked longword or
            quadword transfers can be specified. This memory space uses the onchip write
            buffer more efficiently by condensing multiple quadword transfers into a single
            burst.

6.2.7.1 Generating PCI Addresses for Dense Space
            Dense memory space uses the unshifted CPU address bits a<31:0> to generate
            the PCI address. Because this region has sufficient address space, the
            IOC_HAE register is not needed or used to generate PCI addresses. Note that
            any PCI address can be generated from dense space. Sparse space address
            generation must follow the address extension described in Section 6.2.6.2.

            During CPU-initiated transfers to dense memory space, the IOC forces PCI
            address bits ad<1:0> to 00. This indicates to the target device that the transfer
            will use a linearly incrementing burst order.

            Because the address is not shifted, masked transfers are not permitted in
            dense space.

                                                                                                               I/O Controller 6-13
6.2.7.2 Unmasked Read Operations to Dense Space
            The PCI returns an aligned quadword in response to a longword read
            operation, regardless of the address alignment. The correct longword is
            returned to the register file in the low 32 bits.
            The PCI returns an aligned quadword in response to a quadword read
            operation. The address is assumed to be quadword aligned.

6.2.7.3 Unmasked Burst Write Operations to Dense Space
            corresponding to the data transfer that caused the parity error. If the IOC
            samples the perr_l signal asserted two cycles after a data transfer, it uses an
            internal interrupt to report a parity error to the CPU (Section 6.2.13.2).

6.2.8 I/O Space Cycles

            The IOC generates addresses in this region for transactions to the I/O region
            specified by the PCI architecture. This region supports the same access types
            and masked-address generation as sparse space (Section 6.2.6), with the
            following exceptions:

            � I/O space is accessed with CPU address bits a<33:32> = 01 and
                  a<31:29> = 110.

            � PCI address bits ad<31:27> = 00000 and the IOC_HAE register is not
                  used.

            � PCI address bits ad<1:0> come directly from physical address bits a<6:5>
                  during CPU-initiated PCI transfers to I/O space.

            � An I/O read or write command (Table 6-2) is issued on the c_be_l<3:0>
                  pins coincident with a read or write operation to this region.

            The I/O region supports all masked and unmasked transfers that are supported
            in sparse space.

6.2.9 Configuration Cycles

static void va_debug(va_list argp, const char *fmt)
{
	char buf[DEBUG_BUFSIZE + 1];
	buf[0] = buf[DEBUG_BUFSIZE] = 0;
	vsnprintf(buf, DEBUG_BUFSIZE, fmt, argp);

	char* s = buf;
	while (*s) {
		if (debug_old_currently_at_start_of_line) {
			for (size_t i = 0; i < debug_indent * DEBUG_INDENTATION; i++)
				printf(" ");
		}

		printf("%c", *s);

		debug_old_currently_at_start_of_line = false;
		if (*s == '\n' || *s == '\r')
			debug_old_currently_at_start_of_line = true;
		s++;
	}
}


/*
 *  debugmsg_va():
 *
 *  Used internally by debugmsg().
 */
static void debugmsg_va(struct cpu* cpu, int subsystem,
	const char *name, int verbosity, va_list argp, const char *fmt)
{
	bool subsystem_breakpoint = false;

	/*
	 *  #261: opt-in global break on any ERROR-level message (covers every
	 *  subsystem, incl. ones registered after a per-subsystem breakpoint
	 *  was set). Default off = single false test = no-op.

			about_to_enter_single_step = true;

		show_decorations = false;

The key detector gap is clear: moving the failure check below packing still leaves Alpha’s destination register unchanged. That mutant needs a handler-buffer check with a distinctive byte supplied on failure. I’m also separating ordinary stores from loads, because an unchanged source register cannot show whether a store was declined.
